		<title>Reducing Lithography Learning Cycles To Accelerate FOPLP Ramps</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/reducing-lithography-learning-cycles-to-accelerate-foplp-ramps/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/reducing-lithography-learning-cycles-to-accelerate-foplp-ramps/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[John Chang]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 08:08:35 +0000</pubDate>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>How yield prediction technology can reduce trial-and-error in advanced packaging lithography.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/reducing-lithography-learning-cycles-to-accelerate-foplp-ramps/">Reducing Lithography Learning Cycles To Accelerate FOPLP Ramps</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>For manufacturers scaling advanced AI packages, pressure is mounting from every direction.</p>
<p>On one side, demand continues to rise.</p>
<p>While on another, AI packages are growing larger and integrating more high-bandwidth memory (HBM) and high-performance chips.</p>
<p>And that’s to say nothing of the production teams who face increasing pressure to ramp new processes quickly.</p>
<p>While achieving high yield remains a critical goal, success increasingly depends on how quickly manufacturers can establish stable process conditions and transition from development to high-volume manufacturing (HVM).</p>
<p>Fan-out panel-level packaging (FOPLP) has emerged as an attractive path forward for manufacturers looking to scale advanced packaging production.</p>
<p>Compared with traditional wafer-based approaches, FOPLP can significantly improve substrate utilization while increasing the number of packages produced per substrate. In one study comparison, a 300mm wafer achieved approximately 55% substrate utilization with six packages per wafer, while a 300mm panel achieved 86% utilization with 12 packages per panel and a 510 × 515mm panel achieved 74% utilization with 30 packages per panel. This enables manufacturers to improve manufacturing efficiency and reduce costs per package.</p>
<p>However, realizing those benefits requires overcoming significant lithography challenges.</p>
<p>As panel sizes increase, manufacturers must contend with pattern distortion, die shift, and resulting overlay errors across the substrate. These effects directly impact yield and make process optimization substantially more difficult.</p>
<p>Unlike traditional wafer-based processes, panel-scale variation does not behave uniformly. Instead, distortion patterns can vary significantly across the panel, creating localized challenges that are difficult to correct using conventional approaches.</p>
<h2>The sweet spot</h2>
<p>Ultimately, process engineers must identify the “sweet spot” between overlay yield and throughput. To compensate for panel-level distortion, manufacturers typically rely on a range of correction strategies.</p>
<p>Global correction maintains throughput but often leaves localized overlay errors uncorrected. Zone correction can improve yield, with field data in our study indicating yields between 40% and 80%, but at reduced throughput. Die-by-die correction can further improve yield but introduces a significant throughput penalty. Site-by-site correction offers a compromise, balancing yield improvement with higher throughput.</p>
<p>However, finding that balance is rarely straightforward.</p>
<p>Identifying the optimal operating window often requires repeated experimentation across variables, such as exposure field configurations, die layouts, panel layouts, and overlay thresholds. Each adjustment can require another trial run and another round of data analysis. The result is increased engineering effort, additional material consumption, longer development cycles, and delayed production ramps.</p>
<p>For manufacturers under pressure to bring capacity online quickly, every additional iteration delays the path to production.</p>
<h2>Moving beyond trial-and-error</h2>
<p>By combining offline metrology data with yield prediction algorithms, manufacturers can evaluate process performance before committing to extensive physical trials. Instead of repeatedly testing possible operating conditions on production hardware, engineering teams can assess likely yield outcomes in advance and identify promising process windows earlier in the development cycle.</p>
<p>The process begins with detailed metrology measurements that capture panel-scale variation, including pattern errors and die shifts. Yield prediction algorithms then evaluate this information alongside process variables, such as exposure field configuration, panel layout, die layout, and overlay thresholds. Using these inputs, manufacturers can estimate expected yield performance and compare alternative process strategies without executing full production experiments.</p>
<p>The value extends beyond simple prediction. Visualization tools such as heat maps, vector plots, histograms, and yield dashboards help engineers understand process sensitivities, isolate sources of variation, and identify root causes more quickly (Figure 1). This enables more targeted process optimization and faster decision making.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" fetchpriority="high" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283919" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Onto_Litho-Learning-Cycles-FOPLP-Ramps-fig1.jpg?resize=743%2C424&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="743" height="424" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Onto_Litho-Learning-Cycles-FOPLP-Ramps-fig1.jpg?w=743&amp;ssl=1 743w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Onto_Litho-Learning-Cycles-FOPLP-Ramps-fig1.jpg?resize=300%2C171&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Onto_Litho-Learning-Cycles-FOPLP-Ramps-fig1.jpg?resize=600%2C342&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 743px) 100vw, 743px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Yield prediction analysis tools showing 1) parameters for analysis, 2) X-axis overlay error and 3) Y-axis overlay error. A comparison of these maps reveals that the Y-axis exhibits the most significant overlay errors, which directly contribute to yield loss. This insight enables users to pinpoint the root cause of the issue and take corrective actions effectively, thereby improving overall process performance.</strong></p>
<p>Yield prediction technology has also been evaluated using production substrates in an HVM process. In the initial assessment, differences between predicted and measured yield ranged from 1.2% to 2.7%. Further analysis showed that variability in manual inspection judgments was the primary source of the discrepancies. After the inspection process was standardized, prediction errors were reduced to 0.2% to 0.8%.</p>
<h2>Accelerating qualification</h2>
<p>During process development, manufacturers can use yield prediction technology to evaluate process conditions through simulation before running qualification substrates (Figure 2). Rather than relying exclusively on physical experimentation, engineers can evaluate multiple parameter combinations, identify promising operating conditions, and then use qualification substrates to validate the predictions. This approach can reduce trial-and-error iterations while accelerating process refinement and shortening development timelines.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283920" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Onto_Litho-Learning-Cycles-FOPLP-Ramps-fig2.png?resize=294%2C272&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="294" height="272" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Residual overlay error vector maps generated under various process conditions help accelerate process refinement, reduce trial-and-error iterations and improve efficiency during R&amp;D.</strong></p>
<p>Just as important, it helps engineering teams focus their efforts on optimization rather than repetitive trial-and-error experimentation.</p>
<h2>Extending yield prediction to mass production</h2>
<p>The benefits of yield prediction technology can also extend beyond process development. In mass production, the technology can be integrated into the <a href="https://ontoinnovation.com/product-categories/lithography/">lithography workflow</a> for real-time, in-line process monitoring and earlier defect detection.</p>
<p>A standard lithography workflow includes multiple process steps before final inspection determines overlay yield. Errors introduced along the way can result in rework or permanent yield loss. By integrating yield prediction earlier in the workflow, manufacturers can identify potential problems before final inspection, enabling earlier intervention while reducing the manpower, time and costs associated with rework (Figure 3).</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283921" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Onto_Litho-Learning-Cycles-FOPLP-Ramps-fig3.jpg?resize=743%2C354&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="743" height="354" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Onto_Litho-Learning-Cycles-FOPLP-Ramps-fig3.jpg?w=743&amp;ssl=1 743w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Onto_Litho-Learning-Cycles-FOPLP-Ramps-fig3.jpg?resize=300%2C143&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Onto_Litho-Learning-Cycles-FOPLP-Ramps-fig3.jpg?resize=600%2C286&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 743px) 100vw, 743px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 3: Yield prediction technology can be integrated into the production workflow to support real-time, in-line process monitoring and earlier defect detection.</strong></p>
<p>This extends the role of yield prediction from accelerating process development to supporting process monitoring and early defect detection during mass production.</p>
<h2>Conclusion</h2>
<p>As demand for advanced AI packages continues to rise, manufacturers face growing pressure to establish stable processes, protect yield, and move from development to HVM more quickly. FOPLP offers compelling economic advantages, but pattern distortion, die shift, and yield/throughput tradeoffs can lengthen process learning cycles and delay production ramps.</p>
<p>Yield prediction technology gives manufacturers a way to address these challenges from process development through mass production. During development, this technology can reduce trial-and-error iterations and accelerate process refinement. In mass production, real-time, in-line monitoring can support earlier defect detection and intervention. By incorporating these capabilities into their FOPLP lithography strategies, manufacturers can spend less time finding the right process conditions and advance more efficiently toward stable, high-yield production.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/reducing-lithography-learning-cycles-to-accelerate-foplp-ramps/">Reducing Lithography Learning Cycles To Accelerate FOPLP Ramps</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Test Struggles To Keep Pace With AI</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/test-struggles-to-keep-pace-with-ai/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/test-struggles-to-keep-pace-with-ai/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ed Sperling]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 08:07:24 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Test, Measurement and Analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Advantest]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[chiplets]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[PDF Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Siemens EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[silent data corruption]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Teradyne]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[Vinci]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283944</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Rapidly changing workloads, more test insertions, and fragmented data complicate chip testing.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/test-struggles-to-keep-pace-with-ai/">Test Struggles To Keep Pace With AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>AI workloads are changing faster than chips can be designed, manufactured, and tested, forcing chipmakers to rethink test strategies.</li>
<li>More complex packaging and additional test insertions are generating vast amounts of data, much of it siloed across companies and facilities.</li>
<li>Chipmakers need better ways to monitor aging and reliability as customers push to extend the life of increasingly expensive AI hardware.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>The rapid pace of change in AI models and approaches is creating confusion over what to test, when to test it, and what to monitor once chips are in the field.</p>
<p>Until recently, test targets and the timing for test insertions generally were determined by fab processes and the laws of physics, and the results of those tests were deterministic and quantifiable. But starting with the rollout of generative AI and the ensuing explosion of data centers, the gap has widened between the rollout of new algorithms and use models and the time it takes to deploy a chip in the field.</p>
<p>An increasing number of high-performance processors used in data centers, and many at the edge, are multi-die assemblies. The chips are more complex and take longer to design, manufacture, package, and assemble, and the workloads for which they are designed often change by the time those chips leave final test. Along the way, there are more test insertions along the way, each of which generates more data.</p>
<p>The tests are more complex and varied, as well. In addition to electrical tests, these chips need to be analyzed for mechanical integrity. AI workloads can stress one part of a multi-die assembly more than others, and they can change quickly as algorithms and use cases evolve. That, in turn, requires increasingly complex multiphysics simulations and more real-time monitoring of processor and memory utilization, thermal gradients, and datapath throughput, along with accelerated aging that can affect signal integrity.</p>
<p>&#8220;It&#8217;s a huge challenge,&#8221; said Scott Segura, vice president of sales at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/advantest-corporation/">Advantest</a>. &#8220;People are starting to put more active components into the substrate, and moving things closer to each other, as well, so it&#8217;s turning into more test insertions. What shifts left? What shifts right? There are substrate testers today that are just trying to make sure you have contact, but now we&#8217;re seeing functional testing moving into substrate testing. And at the die level, we&#8217;re probing performance. We&#8217;ve got to control thermal. For known good dies, they have to be able to handle 3 kilowatts at the die level, so now we&#8217;re testing singulated dies. There&#8217;s going to be a shift in how you slice up where things go, and packaging is the key. As you&#8217;re putting more and more stuff in the package, into the substrates, whatever you&#8217;re stacking this stuff on, you&#8217;re going to have to start testing those different steps. Silicon photonics has five insertions right now.&#8221;</p>
<p>It only gets more challenging from here with the transition from wafers to panels, which will require entirely new equipment and processes. At the same time, packaging and assembly, which used to be done in one location, is now being split between fabs and OSATs. That creates ongoing challenges around who sees what data, when they see it, and how much of it can be shared with other companies.</p>
<p>&#8220;Test insertions typically are done at different locations,&#8221; said Jeorge Hutarte, senior director of product marketing in the Semiconductor Test group at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/teradyne-corporation/">Teradyne</a>. &#8220;For example, from wafer sort to singulated die probe could be done at a fab. They have their data collection workflows. They have their own test programs. And they gather failure mechanisms as a result of the testing done at wafer sort and singulated die probing. When it comes to the next step, which is where we put together a compute die plus a stack of HBMs, plus perhaps other chiplets into an interposer — and it&#8217;s going to panels today from round wafers to optimize the utilization of equipment and putting as many dies as possible on a 310 × 310mm panel — this wafer/panel interposer substrate test will likely be done by the OSAT. That&#8217;s a different location with a different test program and a different data collection environment or workflow. They don&#8217;t necessarily talk to the previous test insertion data. And unfortunately in the fabless world, people don&#8217;t share data unless the customer owns everything.&#8221;</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283946" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-05-at-9.26.53-AM-scaled.png?resize=2560%2C1105&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="2560" height="1105" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-05-at-9.26.53-AM-scaled.png?w=2560&amp;ssl=1 2560w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-05-at-9.26.53-AM-scaled.png?resize=300%2C130&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-05-at-9.26.53-AM-scaled.png?resize=1024%2C442&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-05-at-9.26.53-AM-scaled.png?resize=768%2C332&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-05-at-9.26.53-AM-scaled.png?resize=1536%2C663&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-05-at-9.26.53-AM-scaled.png?resize=2048%2C884&amp;ssl=1 2048w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-05-at-9.26.53-AM-scaled.png?resize=600%2C259&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 1: Evolving test strategies and emerging test insertions. Source: Teradyne</strong></p>
<p><strong>Testing gets more complicated</strong><br />
It took slightly more than two years from the introduction of ChatGPT in November 2022 to widespread commercial adoption of agentic AI systems. That&#8217;s roughly the time it takes to design, verify, and validate a chip, get that design onto a shuttle in a fab, package it, and integrate that chip into a functional system. But during that time, the industry shifted from arrays of GPUs for massively parallel multiply-accumulate operations to a combination of GPUs, CPUs, and customized and programmable accelerators for orchestrating how agents work and interact. Those involve very different optimization and prioritization schemes — hierarchies that are constantly being re-evaluated as workloads shift.</p>
<p>Hyperscalers and large chipmakers have been pushing EDA vendors to reduce the time it takes to design these mini-systems. But even if the chip design cycle could be shrunk from today&#8217;s 18 to 24 months to as little as 6 months — a stated goal by some large chipmakers — there would still be a significant gap between the time it takes to get to working silicon and the time it takes to roll out new training and inference models. And that could change again if recurrent self-improvement lives up to its promise.</p>
<p>&#8220;We have customers now that will tape out chips, run their models on them, or look at what the end application is, and they&#8217;ll end up not going to market with them,&#8221; said Advantest&#8217;s Segura. &#8220;They&#8217;ll do a derivative of it, or they&#8217;ll do a different version of it. That could change how much memory they&#8217;re stacking on it, how many cores it has, how much cache it has, and the overall architecture. It totally changes depending on whether you&#8217;re training a frontier model or whether it&#8217;s a CPU hosting something. Even our customers sometimes guess wrong. They go through a chip development cycle, get the silicon out, start to run models on it, and they find out the use model has shifted, or they didn&#8217;t put enough memory in, or it&#8217;s too expensive. They&#8217;ve got to be pretty nimble about what they develop, as well. You see a lot of chiplet designs because it lets them scale a different number of cores, for example. If they originally planned for 256 cores, they may only need 192.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Breaking down data silos</strong><br />
The reasons underlying the gap between hardware and software schedules are complex. Algorithms can be updated in hours or days. Chip architectures can take years to optimize. The goal is to make every process step bulletproof. Achieving that requires more testing, more data, and a clear plan for making sense of that data.</p>
<p>&#8220;Not only are there more tools, but they&#8217;re all generating more data — and pretty rich modes of data with lots of images,&#8221; said Jim Schiely, chief of staff for Calibre Semiconductor R&amp;D at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/mentor-a-siemens-business/">Siemens EDA</a>. &#8220;Over time, customers had been building their own local systems for managing revisions, trying to develop context, because you can&#8217;t just have a bunch of models floating around without knowing what the provenance of all that data is.&#8221;</p>
<p>In some cases, this involves multiple companies. In others, it involves different groups within the same company.</p>
<p>One big challenge here is breaking through silos that have been controlling this data. &#8220;There are two aspects to that,&#8221; said Schiely. &#8220;One is on the technical side, and the other has to do with the relationships between the entities that control that data. On the technical side, the good news is that it&#8217;s sort of AI to the rescue. Waiting for everybody to define the format is going to take a long time. But you can very easily take these digital threads of information and turn them into a model or representation with the help of AI tools, even if there&#8217;s a slightly different data model being used by another department. That stuff can be handled more or less automatically. So that&#8217;s an area where some of the old problems might start to dissolve.&#8221;</p>
<p>Battles over who controls critical data intensified in the 1990s, when manufacturing began shifting from vertically integrated companies to foundries. To get designs to work, the foundries instituted restrictive design rules. Those rules have proven very effective for improving yield, but they also limit design freedom. The so-called design rule deck also has become thicker at each new process node, and the amount of data that needs to be processed and analyzed by different groups has ballooned.</p>
<p>&#8220;This needs to go from historical analysis done by a group to analytics that are site-wide or maybe group-wide to now being effectively networked analytics either across your own supply chain, or collaboration with your partners,&#8221; said Kimon Michaels, co-founder and executive vice president of products and solutions at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/pdf-solutions/">PDF Solutions</a>. &#8220;We&#8217;re driving toward how you can have a standard platform that enables the efficient sharing of data and pulling it together and reaching into different facilities, but the obfuscation of the protection of the IP. We&#8217;re not there yet as an industry for sure.&#8221;</p>
<p>The orchestration required by agentic AI has further blurred the lines of what belongs to whom. &#8220;The complexity in our industry is beyond coming up with the latest, greatest model,&#8221; said Michaels. &#8220;It&#8217;s about dealing with the geographical dispersion across the customer-vendor relationship to manage, collect, and understand what model, what data has to be at what place at the right time.&#8221;</p>
<p>This matters because chips are increasingly becoming assemblies of heterogeneous components.</p>
<p>&#8220;You have active components sitting on a substrate that are very difficult to access, so now they&#8217;re building BiST into it,&#8221; Segura said. &#8220;They&#8217;re looking at how different dies are talking to each other. And not just dies. You could have inductors, capacitors, or some type of voltage regulator. How do we self-test that, because it&#8217;s really hard to get access to that on the substrate level? All of that&#8217;s changing how you&#8217;re going to test when you don&#8217;t have access to it. Some of these dies have CPUs in them, so they already have built-in self-test for burn-in. But in cases where you can&#8217;t get access, either you don&#8217;t test it, or you use something that&#8217;s connected to it. It&#8217;s getting more and more complicated, and people are making this up as they go.&#8221;</p>
<p>That was considered business as usual when developing a chip was relatively inexpensive, and field failures were manageable. But in a complex collection of chiplets with HBM memory running mission-critical or safety-critical systems, those costs can quickly spiral out of control. Avoiding them requires more simulations, more testing, and more analysis of live systems.</p>
<p>&#8220;In some cases, because of silent data corruption and other potential defects, you may want to do system-level tests, as well,&#8221; said Teradyne&#8217;s Hutarte. &#8220;Some companies are asking to insert between wafer sort and singulated die a wafer-level burn-in stage, where you can accelerate infant mortality before you actually commit it to the interposer as a stack-up. What&#8217;s changed in the AI accelerator world, as compared to the mobility era, is that every wrong screening translates to thousands of dollars of loss because of failures at the data center. So the trend is toward more and more shift-left screening and added insertions. The test intensity has at least doubled over the AI era, because once they hit the rack assembly, they could be worth $30,000 to $50,000. We cannot throw away $30,000 because of a defective chiplet.&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Longer lifespans, changing workloads</strong><br />
As a result, chipmakers are increasingly looking at rework rather than scrapping dies or full chips, and their customers are looking at extending the life of these chips.</p>
<p>Some of this can be improved with more data, including synthetic data that is being used where real data is unavailable. But more data also requires more planning as to how to use that data effectively.</p>
<p>&#8220;One of the new trends involves workload scheduling,&#8221; said Rahima Mohammed, distinguished engineer and semiconductor technical advisor at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/vinci/">Vinci</a>. &#8220;How do you move the workloads in a way that you don&#8217;t heat it up as much and keep it safe? You also need to really think through all the applications very carefully.&#8221;</p>
<p>Efficiencies can be gained at multiple points in a system-level architecture, whether that&#8217;s access to memory, understanding which data needs to be processed in real-time, and how to parse the processing across a system to ensure that, and how and where to reroute data if data pathways become slower due to electromigration. The list goes on, but all of these decisions also need to take into account structural changes inside customized packaging, the degradation of dielectrics, and the effectiveness of the cooling architecture, any of which can impact how a system behaves over time.</p>
<p>&#8220;When you think about chiplets and the smallest processes, how do the transistors come together? Where is the self-heating coming from? You have to think about this from the nanometer level all the way up to the package level,&#8221; Mohammed said. &#8220;With chiplets, how do you bring those into the package? In a previous time, PCB packaging was just about adding layers. You didn&#8217;t have to think about all the copper lines or vias. We just kind of winged it. You don&#8217;t have that option anymore. You have to get power through all the possible layers that you can think about, whether that&#8217;s horizontal, vertical, or lateral, and you have to do all these &#8216;what ifs.&#8217; If I change the location of the metal lines here, what happens?&#8221;</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion</strong><br />
Test is no longer a fixed step in chipmaking. Historically, test represented a small percentage of manufacturing costs, and test schedules were more predictable. But as leading-edge chips become increasingly heterogeneous collections of chiplets and other components in a customized advanced package with high utilization rates and very specific applications, test is no longer cookie-cutter. It is a unique process that is tailored to a specific design, and one that is increasingly costly and time-consuming. On top of that, the use case for these chips is changing so quickly that by the time a chip is fully tested and ready for work, it may already be outdated.</p>
<p>AI may be used to design better AI chips and help integrate data from various processes, such as test, but keeping up with the changes within AI is a much tougher challenge. And until the technology settles into a pattern of use that isn&#8217;t uprooted every month or so, the chip industry is likely to face some hard choices about how much customization is needed to remain competitive, and how long it&#8217;s expected to last.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/test-struggles-to-keep-pace-with-ai/">Test Struggles To Keep Pace With AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Which Passing Chips Need More Testing?</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/which-passing-chips-need-more-testing/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/which-passing-chips-need-more-testing/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Gregory Haley]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 08:05:20 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Test, Measurement and Analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[adaptive test]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Advantest]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ATE]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[burn-in]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[final test]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Modus Test]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Nordson Test & Inspection]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[outlier detection]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[PDF Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[predictive analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[proteanTecs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[reliability]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[semiconductor test]]></category>

		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283840</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Manufacturers are using test data and predictive models to identify marginal chips and determine when additional screening is justified.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/which-passing-chips-need-more-testing/">Which Passing Chips Need More Testing?</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Chips that pass identical tests can carry very different reliability risks, because marginal behavior often hides inside specification limits.</li>
<li>Device-level models, cross-insertion data, and physical inspection can flag suspicious passing parts, but an anomaly isn’t proof of a defect.</li>
<li>Extra screening pays off only when the added test can expose the suspected weakness at a lower cost than the failures it may prevent.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>A chip passes wafer sort, moves through assembly, and passes final test. Nothing in its results gives anyone a reason to hold it. Yet another chip with nearly identical results could fail in the field because of a weakness neither test exposed, whether that’s a thin timing margin, a slight resistance shift, or a combination of measurements that looks harmless when each is viewed alone.</p>
<p>The challenge for manufacturers is figuring out which of those passing chips deserve another look without retesting everything. Test engineers have always known that a passing result doesn’t guarantee lifetime reliability. What’s changed is how much they can learn about each device. Internal parametric measurements, manufacturing history, and predictive models can now reveal unusual behavior inside a population of passing parts, but spotting a suspicious device is just the beginning.</p>
<p>“The cost of test and the amount of test is exploding, and the ability to test before system-level test is a challenge,” said Kimon Michaels, executive vice president of products and solutions, director, and co-founder of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/pdf-solutions/">PDF Solutions</a>. “But of course, one of the big challenges is how much of your supply chain is at risk at that point, or the amount of product that you’re throwing away if it fails at that point.”</p>
<p>The longer a marginal device goes undetected, the more value gets invested in it. But testing every device more thoroughly isn’t free. Extra patterns eat tester time, burn-in ties up equipment, a packaged device may need another mechanical insertion, and rejecting every statistical outlier throws away some good silicon. On the other side of the ledger is the part that escapes into a customer’s system.</p>
<p>“Nothing is more expensive than a field failure,” said Michaels. “The challenge is that the advanced technologies, in some respects, are even more sensitive to reliability failures.”</p>
<p style="font-weight: 400;">Manufacturing tests can only approximate the conditions a chip will see in operation. Voltage, temperature, timing, and workload are controllable during test, but the interactions inside a fully assembled system are much harder to reproduce.</p>
<p style="font-weight: 400;">“Individual dies can pass wafer sort and final test, and then behave differently once the dies, memory devices, interconnects, firmware, and software are all working together,” said Eli Roth, smart manufacturing product manager at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/teradyne-corporation/">Teradyne</a>. “We’re very good at finding hard failures. What’s much harder is finding devices that meet spec but are operating close to a reliability boundary.”</p>
<p>Testing every combination of operating conditions is impractical, and even extensive system-level tests cannot guarantee that every weakness will surface. Manufacturers need a way to identify high-risk devices and decide whether a targeted test can expose the problem before shipment.</p>
<p><strong>Device-level models are exposing hidden outliers</strong><br />
Conventional production testing relies on defined limits, with guard bands for measurement uncertainty and operating variation. Statistical analysis can flag devices near those limits or behaving differently from their neighbors on the same wafer or lot. Nevertheless, a chip can still pass every test while showing an unusual relationship among its measurements.</p>
<p>Device-specific models offer another way to identify those differences. By comparing internal parametric measurements with the behavior expected from a particular device, engineers can detect inconsistencies that population-based statistics might overlook. Process and timing signatures can provide the basis for predicting how a chip should behave, allowing engineers to investigate discrepancies between predicted and measured performance. The pattern of those discrepancies matters. Design-related margin limits tend to show up consistently across a product population, but material problems don’t.</p>
<p>“Material-induced variability behaves differently. It is often localized, asymmetric, or statistically scattered,” said Nir Sever, senior director of business development at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/proteantecs/">proteanTecs</a>. “Only a subset of devices, wafers, or regions on a die may show accelerated drift. The degradation slope may diverge from the population over time.”</p>
<p>Localized or accelerating changes can suggest an emerging physical weakness. The difficulty is determining whether that variation represents a reliability risk worth investigating, and whether another test can reveal anything useful about it. The models making those calls also have a shelf life.</p>
<p>“Data is not static,” said Michaels. “It drifts. It’s physics-based. There are changes to the process, and you have to be cognizant of that in how you retrain, how you monitor, etc.”</p>
<p>Process adjustments, equipment changes, and product revisions can alter the relationships a model learned. That means engineers need to validate the models as carefully as the measurements feeding them, especially when an outlier flag can trigger extra screening or reject a part.</p>
<p><strong>Inspection explains marginal electrical behavior</strong><br />
Some latent defects don’t produce a consistent electrical failure. A weakened interconnect may conduct well enough at room temperature, then become unstable under thermal or mechanical stress. That’s particularly hard to catch in advanced packages. Partial non-wetting, micro-bump voids, delamination, and warpage may not cause an immediate failure.</p>
<p>“Voiding and non-wetting may weaken an interconnect without causing an immediate open,” said Chris Rand, principal technical engineer at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/cyberoptics/">Nordson Test &amp; Inspection</a>. “Variation in bump height, solder volume, or interconnect geometry can shift resistance or create continuity that changes under load, temperature, or handling.”</p>
<p>X-ray or acoustic inspection can provide physical evidence of a suspected interconnect problem, while electrical measurements help establish whether an observed irregularity affects device behavior. Neither technique necessarily provides a complete diagnosis on its own. Correlating the results can narrow the possible failure mechanisms and help engineers select a stress condition more likely to expose the weakness.</p>
<p>Comparing measurements across test insertions offers another clue. A chip may pass at every stage while its electrical characteristics slowly shift, a trend that individual pass/fail decisions would miss.</p>
<p>“An unexplained drift is not a healthy sign,” said Greg Prewitt, director of Exensio Solutions at PDF Solutions. “If a device just changes, and you don’t think it’s a problem with the test setup, it requires more scrutiny.”</p>
<p>Flagging that device and carrying the flag to later insertions lets manufacturers focus extra screening on parts with suspicious histories. But engineers first have to rule out differences in temperature, calibration, contact resistance, or instrument configuration. Otherwise, they risk chasing a healthy device while missing a problem in the measurement path.</p>
<p><strong>Failure mechanisms determine which test comes next</strong><br />
Once the evidence points to a credible failure mechanism, the question becomes which additional test has a reasonable chance of exposing it. The options aren’t interchangeable. Suspicious timing may call for characterization at a different voltage or temperature. An interconnect concern may need electrical or thermal stress, while a reliability-risk signature may justify burn-in. Repeating the original test under the same conditions adds little if that test was never sensitive to the defect in the first place.</p>
<p>Burn-in is a particularly tough call. Elevated temperature and voltage can accelerate certain failure mechanisms, but the process consumes equipment time, power, and capacity, and the stress itself can affect reliability. That makes blanket burn-in hard to justify when only a small fraction of passing devices are at risk.</p>
<p>“People are burning in large fractions of their inventory, or nearly all of it, which is expensive and can cause damage by itself,” said Michaels. “But a fairly broad model can learn the insights as to what multi-dimensional part of your process-based plus your test state predicts, and what the sweet spot is where you can skip it and focus your resources on the more marginal parts where you have concern.”</p>
<p>Predictive burn-in uses manufacturing history and test data to identify devices most likely to benefit from stress screening. Adaptive test takes a related approach, using wafer-level and die-level results to determine how a device should be tested after assembly. Information gathered at one insertion can influence the limits and patterns used at the next, provided the manufacturer can preserve device identity and connect the results across operations.</p>
<p>”We have established a kind of cloud infrastructure that is fetching data from the different test insertions and reusing the data collected at earlier test insertions to optimize the die matching inside advanced packaging, the test conditions, and consequently the yield for the following test insertions,&#8221; said Fabio Pizza, test technology director at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/advantest-corporation/">Advantest</a>. “You can have a kind of adaptive test to make sure that when you test the device after assembly, you are using the right limits and even the right pattern.”</p>
<p>Changing limits or patterns within an existing insertion generally imposes less overhead than routing a packaged device through another operation, which adds handling, equipment demands, and time. The economics become more complicated as additional value is invested in each device. An extra test may be inexpensive compared with scrapping an assembled multi-die package, but every added operation competes for finite manufacturing capacity.</p>
<p>“When you look at the economics, test costs are dwarfed by scrap costs once you start packaging these devices together,” said Teradyne’s Roth.</p>
<p>The challenge is deciding where that additional screening produces enough value to justify its cost. Connecting wafer, assembly, and final-test results can help engineers identify suspicious devices before more manufacturing value is added, while avoiding test operations that contribute little useful information. A few seconds of targeted screening may prevent an expensive downstream failure, but an extra insertion that rarely finds anything can reduce throughput without improving outgoing quality.</p>
<p><strong>Retesting is creating its own uncertainty</strong><br />
Retesting has an obvious appeal. If engineers don’t trust a result, another measurement seems like cheap insurance. But production test involves physical interfaces, and those interfaces aren’t passive. Packaged devices are pressed into sockets with substantial force to make contact across hundreds or thousands of terminals. Contacts wear, surfaces get contaminated, and small seating variations change resistance, so a second insertion may not reproduce the conditions of the first, even when the test program is identical.</p>
<p>“Every time you put that part into the socket, there’s risk involved,” said Jack Lewis, chief technologist at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/modus-test/">Modus Test</a>. “So retest is a problem. You don’t want to be retesting. Test times are long.”</p>
<p>Repeated insertions consume capacity and expose the package to more handling. If the underlying issue is contact-related, a second test may produce a different result without explaining which measurement better reflects the device. A pass after an earlier fail is especially ambiguous. The device may have been healthy all along, with the original failure caused by poor contact, or it may carry an intermittent defect that simply didn’t show up the second time. Changing the bin without keeping the history throws away the information needed to tell those cases apart.</p>
<p>Comparing results across probe pins, boards, and testers can help establish whether a failure belongs to the device or the measurement system. That requires more than routine calibration, particularly when a tester resource or load-board component begins drifting toward its operating limits.</p>
<p>“Customers also want to do some dedicated measurements to monitor if there is any parameter of the complete test cell that is deviating, if there is any resource in the tester, load board, probe card or thermal control system that is going closer to the limit and potentially creating some marginalities when testing such devices,” said Advantest’s Pizza.</p>
<p>Monitoring tester and load-board performance helps engineers distinguish changes in the measurement environment from changes in the silicon. That history becomes particularly valuable when deciding whether a previously passing device needs more screening, because another insertion may reproduce the symptom without identifying its cause. In that case, other evidence has already raised the possibility of latent risk, so the added insertion should have a real chance of revealing the suspected defect, particularly when the part is expensive or a field failure would be severe. A method that catches more marginal devices but sharply increases false rejects, handling damage, or tester occupancy may not be worth it.</p>
<p><strong>Decision latency is limiting what screening can catch</strong><br />
Even a good screening model fails operationally if its answer arrives too late. Some production test operations are completed in fractions of a second, and an analysis completed after the lot has moved on may help future process improvements without changing the disposition of the parts that generated the data.</p>
<p style="font-weight: 400;">Historically, test systems have been optimized to maintain throughput, sometimes at the expense of immediate data availability. Roth described an IDM customer whose test infrastructure deliberately deferred building its test data files until the tester had idle time. That approach kept production moving, but it also limited opportunities to respond to problems as they emerged.</p>
<p style="font-weight: 400;">&#8220;In extreme cases, STDF files aren’t built until the end of a lot,” said Roth. “So if something goes wrong on an early wafer, we’re not collecting or presenting that data in time for anyone to act on it. Historically, that’s been the trade-off in the push for throughput.”</p>
<p>Getting information sooner is only part of the problem. Data must be tied to the correct device, available in usable form, and delivered while the test program or manufacturing system can still respond. Enabling an extra measurement within the current test sequence may be relatively straightforward. Routing a device to another operation, placing it on hold, or evaluating it against data from elsewhere in the flow is more complicated.</p>
<p>The rules governing those actions need attention, too. If a model starts flagging a growing share of passing devices, engineers have to determine whether the process changed, the devices became more marginal, or the model drifted. Automatically ramping up screening in response could consume test capacity without improving quality. The reverse risk applies when a model learns that a particular test rarely finds defects and recommends running it less often.</p>
<p>Establishing a reliable baseline becomes especially difficult with new chip designs. Complex devices generate enormous amounts of measurement data, but engineers may have little historical information against which to judge an unusual result. Without a dependable reference, even a sophisticated model can struggle to distinguish a meaningful anomaly from normal variation.</p>
<p>“Being able to accurately, empirically assess the data and find out what&#8217;s anomalous and what&#8217;s not is by no means an easy task,” said Brent Bullock, test technology director at Advantest. “Engineers may not have reliable historical data for comparison, particularly when testing a new chip design or moving to a different architecture.”</p>
<p>A screening model needs to establish what constitutes normal behavior for the device and operating conditions being evaluated. That baseline must remain valid as manufacturing processes, product revisions, and test conditions change. Otherwise, engineers risk increasing test coverage in response to harmless variation or reducing coverage when a genuine weakness is developing.</p>
<p>Engineers also need to know why a device was selected, what evidence triggered the decision, what the extra test found, and whether the result supported the original risk assessment. Without that feedback loop, manufacturers accumulate screening rules that are expensive to maintain and hard to justify. With it, they can judge screening by actual outcomes rather than by the apparent sophistication of the model.</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion</strong><br />
The decision to release a marginal device comes down to what manufacturers can establish about it and how much uncertainty they’re willing to accept. Flagging a component as at risk isn’t the same as predicting failure. A risk score may justify further characterization, a different stress condition, or closer monitoring of a population, but it doesn’t automatically justify scrapping the part. Some failure mechanisms also remain hard to reproduce under practical production conditions, so even a well-chosen test may reduce uncertainty without eliminating it.</p>
<p>The calculation also varies by market. A supplier of high-volume consumer devices may accept a different balance of test cost and residual risk than one serving automotive, industrial, or other high-reliability applications. Even within a product family, the right response depends on how severe the suspected failure mechanism is and how well the available screening methods can expose it.</p>
<p>As more data becomes available about each device, the hard question is shifting from whether a manufacturer can take another measurement to whether that measurement is likely to change the outcome. Manufacturers have spent decades getting better at determining whether a chip passes. Now they also have to decide which passing chips deserve another look, and when the evidence is strong enough to leave them alone. The better they get at that, the less extra testing they may need.</p>
<hr />
<p><strong>Related Articles</strong><br />

<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/smart-test-collides-with-the-data-chain/">Smart Test Collides With The Data Chain</a><br />
Increasing complexity is limiting the ability of machine learning models to effectively utilize test data.</p>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/easing-the-stress-for-package-level-burn-in/">Easing The Stress For Package-Level Burn-In</a><br />
Employing more stress testing at the wafer level improves quality while reducing burn-in time and cost. So why isn’t it happening?</p>

		<title>AI&#8217;s Efficiency Challenge For The Semiconductor Industry</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/ais-efficiency-challenge-for-the-semiconductor-industry/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/ais-efficiency-challenge-for-the-semiconductor-industry/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[John Kibarian]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 08:03:58 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - PTES]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Bringing AI To Scale]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Test, Measurement and Analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[data analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[efficiency]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[energy efficiency]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[LLMs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[PDF Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[supply chains]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283928</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>AI is reshaping the tools, data, and collaboration models used to design, manufacture, and test silicon.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ais-efficiency-challenge-for-the-semiconductor-industry/">AI&#8217;s Efficiency Challenge For The Semiconductor Industry</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><em>This post is based on John Kibarian’s keynote given at the PDF Solutions CONNECT event on October 15, 2026. The recording and slides of this presentation can be <a href="https://www.pdf.com/users-conference/connect-2026/next-generation-pdf-solutions-platform-for-the-global-semiconductor-industry/">found here</a>.</em></p>
<p>AI is the most consequential shift the semiconductor industry has faced, not because it is driving an upcycle, but because it is changing how the industry works.</p>
<p>The industry has seen big transformations before. Mobile and the internet reshaped what semiconductor companies sold, but they did not change how fabs, fabless companies and their supply chains operated. AI does both. It drives unprecedented demand for silicon, and is simultaneously reshaping the tools, data and collaboration models used to design, manufacture and test silicon.</p>
<p>We began reimagining our portfolio around an AI-first premise roughly four years ago, when ChatGPT first appeared. Today, the result is a next-generation platform that spans three long-standing areas of our work. The first two are accelerating yield ramps for new technologies and applying the latest IT technology to manufacturing data. The final area is connecting manufacturing operations across the supply chain, a thread strengthened by the 2025 acquisition of secureWISE.</p>
<p>Efficiency is today’s watchword. Efficiency is what matters. A manufacturing executive told suppliers recently that he could not get more tools than had already been promised. His only path to meeting demand was getting more out of the tools he had, a sentiment now heard across the industry, and shapes three challenges AI poses for semiconductor manufacturing:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Energy efficiency:</strong> getting more out of the AI data centers being built.</li>
<li><strong>Data and compute efficiency:</strong> leveraging AI to create insights from manufacturing data at an acceptable cost.</li>
<li><strong>Supply chain efficiency:</strong> operating a globally distributed and increasingly complex supply chain.</li>
</ol>
<h2>Challenge 1: Energy efficiency</h2>
<p>Growth numbers are no longer in question, as the semiconductor industry reached a trillion dollars earlier than projected. Looking forward, McKinsey &amp; Company’s base case has device revenue rising from $975 billion in 2025 to about $2.3 trillion in 2030. That’s an 83.5% increase in volume, with 58% of that growth coming from data centers and servers, the one vertical compounding at 29% a year.</p>
<p>Translated into physical capacity, the figures are striking. Installed AI data center capacity of 45–60 GW in 2026 is projected to reach 100–160 GW by 2030. At 150,000–200,000 wafers per gigawatt, half being HBM, that implies 9.6–17.5 million additional wafers and 16–29 new fabs of 50,000 wafer starts per month. This estimate could be conservative, as no company yet builds 50,000-wafer fabs at the leading edge. A modern leading-edge logic fab runs closer to 20,000 wafer starts, so the real fab count could double.</p>
<p>With AI revenue projected at $3.9 trillion in 2030 and margins at roughly $30 million per megawatt, the temptation is to treat this as a capital problem. Nation states look at the numbers and conclude they can write the check. Anyone who has run a fab knows better. Building capacity is only part of the answer. The harder problem is getting more compute out of every data center.</p>
<p>That pressure is already reshaping the landscape, and efficiency is coming from new designs. For years, most of our new customers came from Asia, where fabs and fabless companies were being started. Today, foundation model companies and hyperscalers are designing their own silicon with energy efficiency as the headline metric. Workloads are also being tuned for efficiency. Training keeps a chip saturated at its design point, while a GPU doing inference can spend over 90% of its power on memory updates and idle time.</p>
<p>Using that measure, the industry still has about a factor of a million of headroom, according to Richard Feynman’s lectures from the 1980s on the fundamental limits of computing.</p>
<h3>What that means for manufacturers</h3>
<p>Fabless companies previously could not justify manufacturing software because they were not manufacturers. They are now. What happens after a wafer leaves the front-end fab is where much of the industry’s action now sits.</p>
<p>A few examples illustrate the rapid change. Energy-efficient, bespoke designs carry operational consequences that land squarely on manufacturing and test with smaller production volumes across more products. Custom designs are proliferating rather than a few high-volume parts.</p>
<p>The breadth of new product introduction (NPI) testing carries through into production for accelerated yield ramps with wider datasets and test programs running to millions of test items.</p>
<p>And new technologies such as co-packaged optics and similar integrations mean more than electrons are moving through the production line, requiring new process controls.</p>
<p>PDF Solutions’ portfolio answers these pressures. Characterization Vehicle and DirectScan address co-optimization of design and manufacturing at sub-7nm nodes using more design information to know where and how to look, including e-beam technology designed to find buried systematic defects in 3D structures. Exensio Aurora, Exensio Test Operations and Exensio Scalable Analytics handle rapid analysis of wide test data and deploy rules and AI workflows at the edge. A Python-first, natively parallel compute engine moves analysis from the client to a scalable back end, delivering 20–40x the performance of the same hardware spend through more efficient use of cores. Sapience and Exensio Process Control extend advanced process control, application integration and supply chain orchestration.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283929" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig1.webp?resize=780%2C414&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="780" height="414" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig1.webp?w=780&amp;ssl=1 780w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig1.webp?resize=300%2C159&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig1.webp?resize=768%2C408&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig1.webp?resize=600%2C318&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 780px) 100vw, 780px" /></p>
<h2>Challenge 2: Data and compute efficiency</h2>
<p>According to an interview in The New York Times, Nvidia’s Jensen Huang noted, “Only in the last six months, after 15 years of trying, has AI become useful.” It’s been useful somewhat longer, but the framing raises the right question for semiconductor manufacturing. What does useful mean?</p>
<p>For the semiconductor manufacturing industry, it means combining common-knowledge industry data with company-specific data; efficiently using all available manufacturing data; efficiently managing the compute cost of AI for ongoing analysis; and deploying agents at the manufacturing edge across applications and the supply chain.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283930" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig2.webp?resize=780%2C366&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="780" height="366" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig2.webp?w=780&amp;ssl=1 780w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig2.webp?resize=300%2C141&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig2.webp?resize=768%2C360&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig2.webp?resize=600%2C282&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 780px) 100vw, 780px" /></p>
<p>Each is harder than it looks.</p>
<p>Large language models already know a great deal about semiconductors. Ask one about wafer maps, parametric test, bins, lots, SPC charts, etch-rate drift or failure mechanisms, and it reasons competently. It selects methods, generates code, interprets results and summarizes unstructured data. Give a general-purpose LLM a yield loss in lots and ask what analysis to run and it will produce a decent first pass, roughly what a junior engineer would propose.</p>
<p>The problem is what comes next. A less experienced engineer runs the analysis, sees a signal they cannot explain, dismisses it as noise and reports that there is nothing in the data. A senior engineer looks at the same chart and sees what is going on because they carry context the model does not. What LLMs miss is company-specific data and IP, the historical baseline, product and process specifics such as recipes, design rules, test flows and bin definitions, large volumes of statistical computation, and validation.</p>
<p>Using available data is its own challenge. Our tools worked through many yield crashes, and the post-mortem almost always finds the data was already there. An engineer cannot know which are relevant until there’s an event. Getting efficient at working with wide datasets is what enables a reaction before the crash.</p>
<h3>The scale of private company data</h3>
<p>The most important factor in applying AI to semiconductor manufacturing is accessing and processing complex private company data.</p>
<p>The scale is formidable. Leading fabless and giga-scale foundries hold petabytes of data per site across more than 20 data domains, each with 100 or more file formats. A new 3D-IC can carry 2–10 million test parameters at NPI. Mixed-signal companies that consider their test programs modest, perhaps 10,000 parameters, still accumulate around 200 TB of analytics data. All of them have more products than employees, let alone product and test engineers. At one foundry, the task is analyzing 2–3 trillion sort parameters across roughly 2,000 distinct products every week, with 100,000-plus features per unit.</p>
<p>That’s why PDF Solutions built Exensio Aurora using 30 years of semiconductor manufacturing knowledge for AI context. We have petabytes of manufacturing data managed on isolated customer cloud sites, more than 1,000 test chips taped out for process characterization in the past 15 years, over 660,000 analytics templates and best practices built by customers across those sites. We also have an extensive semiconductor ontology for collecting and aligning data that is never commingled across sites, and neither are the agents that run on it.</p>
<h3>The LLM cost problem</h3>
<p>Tokens have become the single largest and fastest-growing IT expense. One developer ran out of tokens at 2 p.m. The work left in the day would take five hours by hand. Once the cap was lifted, it took five minutes with tokens, illustrating the value and cost of LLM compute.</p>
<p>Manufacturing has a problem with the cost. Manufacturing analysis is repetitive by design, but running an LLM on every analysis is also expensive in a way that does not improve with scale. Consider the goal of repeatedly analyzing a 100,000-row, 10-column data sample:</p>
<table style="border-collapse: collapse;" border="1">
<tbody>
<tr style="background-color: #ddd;">
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Approach</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Tokens</strong></td>
<td style="padding: 5px;"><strong>Energy</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">LLM runs every analysis directly against the data</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">30K per analysis</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">30 Wh per analysis</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">LLM creates the analytical workflow once</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">20K, once</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">20 Wh, once</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 5px;">Stored workflow runs on each new data sample</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">—</td>
<td style="padding: 5px;">0.02 Wh per run</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The repeated run costs of the LLM-created workflow are roughly a thousandth of the energy of a complete LLM approach. For repeatable analytical tasks, LLMs do not provide economies of scale, and the cost of using them for every analysis can be prohibitive.</p>
<h3>LLMs write workflows, workflows do the work</h3>
<p>The conclusion we reached in building Exensio Aurora is that LLMs need a language, and that language is Python expressed as workflows. The approach has four elements:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Align and enrich data with a semiconductor ontology</strong> so the model and engineer share the same definitions of lots, wafers, bins, tests and tools.</li>
<li><strong>Run analysis on a highly scalable compute engine designed for semiconductor data</strong> rather than pulling data out through APIs and asking the LLM to crunch it.</li>
<li><strong>Use LLMs to create workflows and agents.</strong> The model is asked not to perform the analysis but to build it. This is basically code generation, which is what LLMs do best.</li>
<li><strong>Use workflows to encode context and repeatable analysis.</strong> The engineer inspects the result as visual workflow blocks rather than lines of Python, executes it, and if it works, generalizes it and stores it in a library. The next time, the system runs the stored workflow and skips the 20,000 tokens.</li>
</ol>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283931" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig3.webp?resize=780%2C281&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="780" height="281" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig3.webp?w=780&amp;ssl=1 780w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig3.webp?resize=300%2C108&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig3.webp?resize=768%2C277&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig3.webp?resize=600%2C216&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 780px) 100vw, 780px" /></p>
<p>This architecture also air-gaps customer data from the LLM. Customers can experiment with different models, including ones that do not run on premise, without their data leaving the premises. Captured knowledge can be revisited, which is how hallucinations are prevented. The net result is predictability, repeatability and cost efficiency. An engineer cannot keep track of 10 million parameters. An algorithm can.</p>
<h2>Challenge 3: Supply chain efficiency</h2>
<p>Material moves around a great deal. In the world of hybrid packages, all manufacturing is a complex supply chain execution problem. Wafer fabrication, die preparation, wafer bonding, die stacking, TSV and interconnect, advanced packaging, and test and metrology are distinct stages done in a different facility by a different company in a different country.</p>
<p>This is why PDF Solutions acquired secureWISE, the largest secure network connecting fabs and their equipment vendors, deployed in 3nm fabs in and moving exabytes of data between equipment suppliers and factories. Several equipment vendors’ own AI solutions are built on it by collecting data across their installed fleet, drive learning, and push improved algorithms back to the tools.</p>
<p>secureWISE’s differentiation is control. Traffic is double-encrypted and contents of the packets are not shared. The factory team decides what data leaves, when it leaves, what data and algorithms come back in, what virus scanning is applied to anything entering the facility, and keeps a full audit trail of all of it.</p>
<h3>From human collaboration to agentic collaboration</h3>
<p>The semiconductor industry is an industry of collaboration that’s becoming agentic, raising the bar for how tightly, and how securely the industry is interconnected.</p>
<p>secureWISE was created to let humans collaborate. In practice, most traffic on the network, and most of the revenue associated with it is data moving out and software or algorithms moving back. It is more driven by agents and AI than by people. That balance will keep shifting.</p>
<h2>One platform across design, manufacturing and the supply chain</h2>
<p>The efficiency pressure will not go away. Data centers need more compute per megawatt. Manufacturers need more insight per token and per core. Supply chains need to move data and algorithms increasingly between agents as securely as they once moved people.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283932" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig4.webp?resize=780%2C417&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="780" height="417" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig4.webp?w=780&amp;ssl=1 780w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig4.webp?resize=300%2C160&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig4.webp?resize=768%2C411&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/PDF_AI-Efficiency-Challenge-Semi-Industry-fig4.webp?resize=600%2C321&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 780px) 100vw, 780px" /></p>
<p>Our architecture ties together energy efficiency, data and compute efficiency, and supply chain efficiency on one platform. In new-product development, manufacturing, and at supply chain partners such as OSATs, the same stack repeats. Exensio Aurora and enterprise applications run on Sapience with apps and agents on top. In the fab and at the OSAT, Cimetrix connects equipment, including eProbe, to manufacturing systems and enterprise data warehouses. secureWISE provides secure edge deployment and control across all of it, enabling data, rules, workflows and agents to move between sites under the control of the people who own the data.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/ais-efficiency-challenge-for-the-semiconductor-industry/">AI&#8217;s Efficiency Challenge For The Semiconductor Industry</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

		<title>The Definitive AFM Modes Handbook: 2026 Edition</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/the-definitive-afm-modes-handbook-2026-edition/</link>

		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 08:01:35 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Test, Measurement and Analytics]]></category>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>A comprehensive guide to expanding materials research capabilities.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-definitive-afm-modes-handbook-2026-edition/">The Definitive AFM Modes Handbook: 2026 Edition</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Since its development in the early 1980s, atomic force microscopy (AFM) has been used in groundbreaking research, leading to a constant stream of new applications. AFM has allowed researchers to map structures and properties on the micrometer and nanometer levels, which has led to its wide adoption in fields from semiconductors and piezoelectric materials to energy research and biology. As the technology has matured, AFM has given scientists the opportunity to characterize features at ever‑smaller scales, including such firsts as mapping a live HeLa cell and achieving atomic resolution on a crystal lattice, among others. The many advances in AFM technology over the past several decades have aimed to extend measurements to soft and fragile samples, broaden applications beyond mere topographical characterization, and improve measurement consistency and ease of use. These advancements have not been limited to specific experiments or applications but rather have been added to the common analytical toolboxes of researchers across a variety of applications. Central to all these new advances and approaches are AFM modes.</p>
<p>Read <a href="https://www.bruker.com/en/products-and-solutions/microscopes/materials-afm/resource-library/e-book-the-definitive-afm-modes-handbook.html">more here</a>.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-definitive-afm-modes-handbook-2026-edition/">The Definitive AFM Modes Handbook: 2026 Edition</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
