		<item>
		<title>Streaming Scan Network Test Over PCIe</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/streaming-scan-network-test-over-pcie/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/streaming-scan-network-test-over-pcie/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Matthew Knowles]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 08:01:27 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - PTES]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Next-Generation Test]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Test, Measurement and Analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ATE]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[DFT]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[scan test]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Siemens]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Teradyne]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[test]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283912</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Changing test strategies put a premium on how closely the design ecosystem and the test ecosystem line up. </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/streaming-scan-network-test-over-pcie/">Streaming Scan Network Test Over PCIe</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Every generation of AI and data center silicon brings more logic, more cores, and more scan content that has to reach the device for test. Chiplet-based packages compound it, because each die arrives with its own test requirements and its own patterns. DFT and test engineering teams need better solutions to get content to the device fast enough to keep test time and test cost in line.</p>
<p>One such solution is <a href="https://www.siemens.com/en-us/">Siemens</a>’ <a href="https://www.siemens.com/en-us/products/ic/tessent/test/streamingscan/">Tessent Streaming Scan Network</a>, which packetizes scan data so it can be delivered efficiently and shared across cores. Tessent In-System Test builds on this by adding an on-chip controller that accepts deterministic patterns over a standard AXI bus, meaning test content can arrive through a functional interface the device already carries, such as PCIe or USB, rather than through a dedicated test port.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283913" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Teradyne_Streaming-Scan-Network-Test-Over-PCIe-fig1.png?resize=768%2C425&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="768" height="425" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Teradyne_Streaming-Scan-Network-Test-Over-PCIe-fig1.png?w=768&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Teradyne_Streaming-Scan-Network-Test-Over-PCIe-fig1.png?resize=300%2C166&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Teradyne_Streaming-Scan-Network-Test-Over-PCIe-fig1.png?resize=600%2C332&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: In-System Test Controller (ISTC). Source: Teradyne</strong></p>
<p>Utilizing this approach with <a href="https://www.teradyne.com/products/ultraflexplus/">Teradyne UltraFLEXplus</a> results in a combined solution leveraging a programming model test engineers already use. Teradyne has integrated Siemens Tessent Silicon Executable into the UltraPort server and the <a href="https://www.teradyne.com/resources/ig-xl-software/">IG-XL software</a> on UltraFLEXplus. Content generated in the Tessent shell flow is delivered to the device over the device’s own PCIe interface through UltraPort, and the test is set up, executed, and debugged inside the standard IG-XL programming model. Failure data returns in the formats the Tessent diagnosis ecosystem already consumes, so established yield and failure analysis flows carry straight over.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283914" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Teradyne_Streaming-Scan-Network-Test-Over-PCIe-fig2.webp?resize=768%2C362&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="768" height="362" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Teradyne_Streaming-Scan-Network-Test-Over-PCIe-fig2.webp?w=768&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Teradyne_Streaming-Scan-Network-Test-Over-PCIe-fig2.webp?resize=300%2C141&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Teradyne_Streaming-Scan-Network-Test-Over-PCIe-fig2.webp?resize=600%2C283&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 768px) 100vw, 768px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Tessent Silicon Executable running on the UltraPort Server. Source: Teradyne</strong></p>
<p>Teradyne and Siemens EDA have been building joint solutions since 2000, when built-in self-test first met automatic test equipment. In 2019, the two companies simplified silicon bring-up and debug on ATE through Tessent ATE-Connect and Teradyne’s <a href="https://www.teradyne.com/2022/10/05/teradynes-portbridge-simplifying-the-path-from-design-to-test/">PortBridge</a>. More recently the work has centered on running Streaming Scan Network content on UltraFLEXplus. Each step has followed the same principle: design-for-test decisions and test execution should not be two separate conversations held by two separate teams many months apart.</p>
<p>For a team adopting SSN over a high-speed interface, the practical gains are continuity and support. One programming model carries from engineering bring-up through production. Patterns and diagnosis flows established during design stay usable at the tester. And when something needs attention, the hardware, the embedded software, and the integration between them are supported as one robust solution.</p>
<p>With this solution:</p>
<ul>
<li>Scan content moves over a high-bandwidth interface the device already carries, which keeps test time in line as pattern volume climbs.</li>
<li>Bring-up and debug move faster, because patterns can be patched and re-run inside the same flow that generated them, and failure data comes back in the formats the diagnosis tools have already read.</li>
<li>Deterministic EDT patterns can be applied at ATE, SLT and in-system, so pattern content can be updated as new fault models emerge.</li>
<li>The DFT work a design team has already done in Tessent carries forward to the tester, to the bench, and into the system, with one place to go when something needs support.</li>
</ul>
<p>The AI buildout has changed what a test strategy has to account for. Devices are larger, draw more power, and are increasingly assembled from chiplets, where a single weak die can compromise a package worth many times its own value. Decisions that set test cost are being made earlier, inside the design flow, by teams choosing DFT architecture long before a device reaches a tester. That shift left puts a premium on how closely the design ecosystem and the test ecosystem line up. When the DFT tool and the test platform are engineered to the same interfaces, the work a team does on the design side converts directly into production capability, and the path from architecture decision to tested silicon stays short.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/streaming-scan-network-test-over-pcie/">Streaming Scan Network Test Over PCIe</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Laura Peters]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 08:01:24 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Special Reports]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[3D integration]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[3D X-ray]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[X-ray laminography]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[XRDI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ZEISS Microscopy]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Zygo]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283860</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Existing inspection tools struggle to capture defects, especially those hidden at the bond interface.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/shrinking-hybrid-bonding-pitches-push-inspection-limits/">Shrinking Hybrid Bonding Pitches Push Inspection Limits</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>As interconnect pitches between hybrid bonds approach 5 µm and below, 50 nm particles and surface contamination, as well as nanometer-scale topography variation, can cause bonding failures. Inspection tools must distinguish yield-killing defects from harmless variations.</li>
<li>Pre-bond surface planarity and cleanliness are critical to yield. CMP must control copper recess to just a few nanometers while minimizing roughness, scratches, and particles. Chip manufacturers turn to optical inspection, interferometry, AFM, and atomic force profilometry to characterize local and wafer-scale topography.</li>
<li>Detecting buried defects after bonding remains one of the most significant challenges. Infrared, X-ray, and acoustic imaging can identify voids, delamination, and alignment errors, but each has limitations in resolution, sensitivity, penetration depth, or throughput.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>Hybrid bonding represents a paradigm shift in advanced packaging, replacing 35 µm off-chip microbumps with 10 µm pitch or smaller pad-to-pad interconnects on-chip. It can be used to join silicon-to-silicon, as in DRAM-DRAM bonding in future HBM devices, or even disparate materials like GaN-on-silicon.</p>
<p>This elegant process, which is available on wafer-to-wafer or die-to-wafer platforms, reduces signal delay to negligible levels while enabling smaller, thinner packages with unmatched memory/processor speeds and bandwidth. It also consumes significantly less power.</p>
<p>Hybrid bonding’s success depends on the performance of advanced bonding equipment, as well as metrology’s ability to verify critical alignment, cleanliness, planarity, and surface properties — both before and after bonding. Solving these challenges is proving essential to realizing the vision of true 3D integration at the bonded-chip level.</p>
<p>But as hybrid bonding pitches continue to scale down below today’s production levels of 4 to 9 µm, some inspection and metrology methods are struggling to meet the spatial resolution and sensitivity needed to catch all relevant defect types, especially ones buried at the bond interface. Particles, voids, overlay errors, roughness, CMP defects, and warpage threaten the bond quality between chips. Even a single particle can delaminate the top wafer from the one below, resulting in catastrophic failure.</p>
<p>At times, conventional metrology fails to differentiate a killer defect from minor topographical variation. A handful of tools, including non-destructive optical, IR, X-ray, profilometry, AFM, and acoustic inspection tools, are working to resolve critical defects smaller than 100 nm. These are some of the most stringent requirements ever imposed on a semiconductor process.</p>
<p>“Particles and surface contamination are among the most critical defects in hybrid bonding, as they can prevent contact between bonding surfaces and lead to the formation of voids,” said Burhan Ali, product marketing director at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/onto-innovation/">Onto Innovation</a>. “Smaller interconnect pitches reduce the tolerance for defects, making even nanoscale particles capable of causing bonding voids. For leading-edge hybrid bonding processes, defect size requirements can be as low as 50 nm.”</p>
<p>Cracks and delamination can be particularly insidious problems, especially in the early stages of process development, but also in full-scale production. “For cracks and delamination, acoustic micro-imaging (AMI) using ultrasound in the 200MHz range provides the highest resolution,” said Bryan Schackmuth, AMI product manager at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/cyberoptics/">Nordson Test &amp; Inspection</a><strong>.</strong> “Ultrasound does not travel through air gaps that have a thickness down to about 0.1 µm.  If a crack or delamination is around the 5 µm to 10 µm size (x and y) and is at least 0.1 µm thick (z), there is a good chance that it can be detected with acoustic micro-imaging.”</p>
<p>Figure 1, below, shows some of the most challenging defects to capture.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283861" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-08-at-10.55.50-AM.png?resize=1712%2C954&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1712" height="954" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-08-at-10.55.50-AM.png?w=1712&amp;ssl=1 1712w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-08-at-10.55.50-AM.png?resize=300%2C167&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-08-at-10.55.50-AM.png?resize=1024%2C571&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-08-at-10.55.50-AM.png?resize=768%2C428&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-08-at-10.55.50-AM.png?resize=1536%2C856&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-08-at-10.55.50-AM.png?resize=600%2C334&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 1: Hybrid bonding defects and the methods used to capture them. Source: Laura Peters/Semiconductor Engineering</strong></p>
<p><strong>Four pivotal steps</strong><br />
The performance and reliability of hybrid bonds largely depend on the precision and across-wafer uniformity of the four main processes: surface planarization, surface activation, precision alignment, and thermal bonding.</p>
<p>Chemical-mechanical polishing (CMP), which combines mechanical and chemical removal, must achieve nanometer-scale control of surface topography and copper-pad recess, with negligible surface roughness (0.5nm RMS). Otherwise, dielectric-to-dielectric voids can form during bonding.</p>
<p>Wafer-to-wafer hybrid bonding begins with the wafer processed to the final BEOL interconnect level. Next, a suitable dielectric (either SiCN or SiO2) is deposited and then etched to create vias to the metal below. Next, barrier and copper seed layers are deposited, followed by copper plating. Copper pads are typically square. Copper CMP then polishes the overburden to provide a slight dishing (several nm) below the planar feature. That minuscule gap will be filled when the copper expands during annealing. Next, wafer cleaning removes all contaminants.</p>
<p>Next, the exposed dielectric is activated in a plasma vacuum chamber to create an optimized bonding surface with many dangling Si-O<sup>–</sup> bonds. This is followed by a DI water rinse to hydrate the dielectric. Face-to-face (or face-to-back) bonding of the second wafer, processed up through copper CMP in the same manner as wafer 1, is then aligned with and bonded to wafer 1. Then the wafer pair is brought into intimate contact to form the initial bond, followed by furnace annealing at 350° C for two hours to expand the copper and form permanent Cu-Cu and dielectric-dielectric bonds.</p>
<p><strong>Pre-bond inspection</strong><br />
Prior to bonding, CMP needs to be fine-tuned to provide a flat dielectric surface with a specific run-off profile at the wafer edge. Improper edge roll-off can distort alignment marks, leading to poor contact or voids, which compromise bond strength. The roll-off region must be perfectly smooth and free of microcracks, scratches, or handling damage.</p>
<p>Before micro-inspections, a full-wafer macro-inspection is often performed to catch scratches, large particles, or wafer edge damage. Macro-inspection complements micro-inspection findings and creates a detailed wafer map for every wafer in the lot. Scanning of every single wafer provides the necessary record-keeping for mission-critical devices.</p>
<p>“By inspecting 100% of the wafers at each mask level, our macro-inspection tool provides a great complement to the micro-inspection tools on the market,” said Errol Akomer, applications director at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/microtronic/">Microtronic</a>. “Because they&#8217;re slow, they typically only sample each lot, looking at maybe two to five wafers per lot, so in many cases, they&#8217;re missing some really big defects that could affect the long-term reliability of the chips. In some applications, like data centers or aerospace, the customers may select only the best chips that come out of the lot. They don&#8217;t use the marginal ones that we like to call the walking wounded.”</p>
<p>After cleaning, automated optical inspection (AOI) is usually performed using brightfield and darkfield modes. Darkfield inspection, which collects scattered light from defects, is particularly effective for particles and scratches, while brightfield, which collects reflected light, is useful for isolating residual material like slurry, pattern defects, and topographical surface damage.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283862" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB2.png?resize=842%2C244&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="842" height="244" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB2.png?w=842&amp;ssl=1 842w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB2.png?resize=300%2C87&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB2.png?resize=768%2C223&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB2.png?resize=600%2C174&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 842px) 100vw, 842px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 2: Submicron defects prior to hybrid bonding must be accurately classified to distinguish nuisance defects from killer defects. Source: Onto Innovation </strong></p>
<p>Topographical variations, both local and global, affect bondability. “In advanced processes, localized topography can be more yield-critical, but both global and localized height variations are important in hybrid bonding because they create different process failure mechanisms,” explained Maruko Wu, senior director of product marketing at Onto Innovation. “Interferometric technologies are generally applicable to measure wafer-level or die-level planarity, while OCD (optical CD) and AFM (atomic force microscope) are the best approaches for enabling extremely fine control of just a few nanometers local height variations. White-light or phase-shifting interferometry, as well as AFM, are used to measure copper recess levels.”</p>
<p>White-light or phase-shifting interferometry tools are provided by Bruker, KLA, Onto Innovation, and Zygo, and some companies offer double-sided inspection systems.</p>
<p>“Pre-bond defect inspection is commonly performed using advanced optical inspection systems, which offer high-throughput, full-wafer inspection capabilities, and can detect surface defects and particles with high sensitivity,” said Ali. The details of copper pad morphology require AFMs. “Surface roughness and copper pad topography are typically characterized using AFM, which provides nanometer- to sub-nanometer-scale resolution for accurate measurement of surface features such as roughness, dishing, and protrusion.”</p>
<p>AFMs are finding increasing application in the hybrid bonding process, particularly when just one nanometer of material can make a significant difference in determining whether or not the copper-copper pads fuse together during annealing. After CMP, the copper pads are recessed just 2 nm to 5 nm below the dielectric. Process control needs to keep this specified thickness within 2 nm across the wafer.</p>
<p>Both AFM and atomic force profilometry (AFP) are critical for surface characterization and process optimization. AFM delivers localized, high-resolution surface measurements of recess depth while illuminating pad divots or protrusions (see figure 3). AFP provides complementary large-area topography scans spanning nanometer-scale features to full wafer dimensions, including the bevel edge. AFP can reveal excessive topography at different points across a 33 × 26 mm large-area scan (see Figure 4), even when local AFM scans confirm in-spec dielectric roughness and copper pad recess. AFP is also a useful tool for monitoring wafer edge roll-off.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283863" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB3.png?resize=868%2C402&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="868" height="402" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB3.png?w=868&amp;ssl=1 868w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB3.png?resize=300%2C139&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB3.png?resize=768%2C356&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB3.png?resize=600%2C278&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 868px) 100vw, 868px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 3: AFM topography scans after three different CMP steps produce different recess depths. Source: Bruker</strong></p>
<hr />
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283864" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB4.png?resize=574%2C496&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="574" height="496" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB4.png?w=574&amp;ssl=1 574w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/HB4.png?resize=300%2C259&amp;ssl=1 300w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 574px) 100vw, 574px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 4: Atomic force profilometry shows out-of-spec topography, despite earlier AFMs that indicated in-specification copper recess and roughness. Source: Bruker</strong></p>
<p>“We are currently focused on failure analysis and three-dimensional (3D) imaging of existing bonds and potential identification of failed bonds,” said Thomas Rodgers, senior director of market strategy at Zeiss Microscopy. “Though it is outside our application scope, we are often asked to detect voids in the hybrid copper bonding interconnect, which can give insights into reliability and potential failure points.”</p>
<p>The company is also taking a novel approach to solving topographical issues in hybrid bonding. “Our colleagues in the Zeiss Semiconductor Manufacturing Technology business unit developed a solution for measuring and correcting surface planarity by inducing local strain in the wafer prior to backside thinning. We see increasing market interest in this solution, especially as HCB is used on multi-layer stacked die devices such as those seen in high bandwidth memory,” said Rodgers.</p>
<p><strong>Post-bond metrology</strong><br />
After bonding, IR metrology tools verify alignment between the top and bottom copper pads because the infrared beam can penetrate the silicon. Just as too much copper recess on the pads can cause problems, too little recess can also cause problems. “Excessive copper dishing or protrusion can hinder proper Cu-Cu and dielectric-to-dielectric bonding, resulting in degraded electrical connectivity and reduced bond yield. Likewise, elevated surface roughness reduces the effective contact area between bonding surfaces, leading to lower bond strength and increased risk of bonding defects,” said Ali.</p>
<p>“Alignment also can be measured using 3D X-ray, both in classical computed tomography (CT) and laminography modes, giving a clear picture of the alignment and any overlay errors. We have shown some excellent results here with our tool,&#8221; said Rodgers.</p>
<p>For post-bonding, many engineers are turning to high-resolution X-ray imaging to inspect the bond interface, especially during early stages of process development. Advanced X-ray methods, such as laminography, allow 3D sub-micron inspection and quantification of defects, aiding in process optimization and failure analysis. High-resolution X-ray imaging is an essential technique for the development and failure analysis of hybrid bonds. A resolution of 150 nm has been demonstrated. [1]</p>
<p>“XRDI tends to be used for fully automated leading-edge wafer processing where the process steps can be more aggressive, and the cost of wafer loss and disruption to the line is more significant,” said John Wall, site manager at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/bruker/">Bruker</a> JV UK. &#8220;We can detect subsurface critical defects on patterned/metalized wafers with zero edge exclusion. Full wafer inspection can be performed at ~20wph, thus enabling in-line/at-line inspection for these critical defects.”</p>
<p>&#8220;When it comes to detecting cracks, delamination, and warpage, 3D X-ray is the tool of choice,” said Rodgers. &#8220;Our systems can show warpage and planarity of the bonds, while providing 3D image of cracks and voids without physically cutting the sample. Ultimately, this is used for localizing such defects.”</p>
<p>Acoustic microscopy, widely used to detect voids in microbumps, is now being applied to hybrid-bonded wafer stacks.  “Currently, acoustic micro imaging (AMI) inspection is done after the device is fully assembled,&#8221; said Schackmuth. &#8220;With traditional microbumps, the pitch of 20µm to 40µm would normally become the critical defect size for a delamination or void. Hybrid bonding’s pitch in the 5µm to 10µm range drives the need for higher resolution capabilities in the AMI inspection.”</p>
<p>In acoustic imaging, the resolution is a function of the transducer’s wavelength, but at higher frequencies, the acoustic signal has shallower penetration. Higher frequencies can improve lateral resolution to distinguish two closely spaced voids and detect smaller voids, provided the signal-to-noise ratio is acceptable.</p>
<p>Schackmuth described the differences between resolving and detecting voids with AMI. “We can go up to our 165 MHz transducer, which gets you down to a resolution of 12 microns. But there&#8217;s resolution and detection. So if you had a 12-micron defect, 165 MHz could measure it accurately. But if you had a 5-micron defect, we could detect it, but not measure it accurately. So we’re in the process of developing higher frequency transducers to improve that resolution.”</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion</strong><br />
Hybrid bonding is enabling denser, faster, and more power-efficient 3D semiconductor integration, but shrinking interconnect pitches are placing extraordinary demands on inspection and metrology. Particles, surface contamination, copper dishing, roughness, alignment errors, and buried voids can compromise bond quality, electrical performance, and long-term reliability.</p>
<p>To address these issues, manufacturers are combining optical inspection, interferometry, AFM, X-ray imaging, and acoustic microscopy to characterize bonding surfaces before assembly and detect hidden defects afterward. Yet no single technique provides the resolution, sensitivity, wafer coverage, and throughput required to identify every yield-critical defect.</p>
<p>As hybrid bonding scales toward finer pitches, the central challenge is developing faster, more sensitive, and complementary metrology methods that can distinguish yield-killing defects from harmless variations while also supporting high-volume manufacturing.</p>
<p><strong>Reference</strong></p>
<ol>
<li>Dreier, D. Nilsson and S. Tanaka, “High-Resolution 3D X-Ray Imaging of Hybrid Bonding,” 2026 International Conference on Electronics Packaging and Hybrid Bonding Symposium (ICEP-HBS), Hiroshima, Japan, 2026, pp. 267-268, doi: 10.23919/ICEP-HBS69241.2026.11550690.</li>
</ol>

		<title>Overcoming Silicon Photonics Process Control Challenges in the AI Era</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/overcoming-silicon-photonics-process-control-challenges-in-the-ai-era/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/overcoming-silicon-photonics-process-control-challenges-in-the-ai-era/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Onto Innovation]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 08:01:10 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Test, Measurement and Analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Whitepapers]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Onto Innovation]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[process control]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[silicon photonics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[silicon photonics process control]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[SiPh process control]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283897</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>How connected inspection, metrology and software can help manufacturers move SiPh and CPO toward high-volume production.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/overcoming-silicon-photonics-process-control-challenges-in-the-ai-era/">Overcoming Silicon Photonics Process Control Challenges in the AI Era</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>More bandwidth. Lower latency. Less power consumed and less heat generated.</p>
<p>These are three of the challenges facing manufacturers as AI pushes electrical interconnects toward their limits. Silicon photonics (SiPh) and co-packaged optics (CPO) offer a path forward by using light to transmit data rather than relying solely on electrical signals. But realizing those benefits at production scale introduces another challenge: maintaining precise process control across the optical structures, materials and integration processes that ultimately determine device performance and yield.</p>
<p>At the same time, traditional copper interconnects face increasing resistance, electromigration and power challenges as semiconductor architectures scale (Figure 1). For manufacturers, the shift toward silicon photonics places greater importance on precise control of optical structures and materials.</p>
<div id="attachment_7675" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="wp-image-7675 size-full" src="https://i0.wp.com/ontoinnovation.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/overcoming-silicon-figure-1.png?resize=570%2C241&#038;ssl=1" sizes="auto, (max-width: 570px) 100vw, 570px" srcset="https://ontoinnovation.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/overcoming-silicon-figure-1.png 570w, https://ontoinnovation.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/overcoming-silicon-figure-1-300x127.png 300w" alt="" width="570" height="241" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-7675" /></div>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: In the AI era, interconnects (within die and die-to-die and board-to-board) become the bottleneck. Source: Onto Innovation</strong></strong></p>
<p>One of the fundamental differences between conventional semiconductor manufacturing and silicon photonics is what manufacturers need to control.</p>
<p>Photonics yield and performance depend on critical dimensions and tight control of geometry, films and defects. Optical performance can be highly sensitive to relatively small process variations, with consequences for device performance and yield.</p>
<p>That makes several areas particularly important:</p>
<ul>
<li>Fiber connect (V-groove) geometry, which can affect fiber placement, alignment and coupling efficiency.</li>
<li>Waveguide geometry and cladding, where height, width, sidewall roughness and taper, refractive index and cladding thickness influence light confinement and propagation, along with defects located on the waveguide facet itself.</li>
<li>Microlenses, where defects, shape, placement and material nonuniformity can degrade transmission, beam shaping and coupling.</li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li>Laser devices, which require control of geometry, films and defectivity.</li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li>Die attach, die level bow/warp, micro bumping, through silicon vias (TSVs) and related integration structures, where geometry and defects can affect optical and electrical performance.</li>
</ul>
<p>The result is a process control challenge that extends across multiple inspection and metrology steps. Manufacturers need visibility into the different variables that influence optical performance throughout fabrication and integration.</p>
<p>Read <a href="https://ontoinnovation.com/resources/overcoming-silicon-photonics-process-control-challenges-in-the-ai-era/">more here</a>.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/overcoming-silicon-photonics-process-control-challenges-in-the-ai-era/">Overcoming Silicon Photonics Process Control Challenges in the AI Era</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>LPDDR6 becomes an essential ingredient as intelligence moves into autonomous and mobile edge systems.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/memory-in-physical-ai/">Memory In Physical AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Humanoid robots, autonomous vehicles, and drones all share one thing in common — they can only function as long as the battery lasts. After that, they need to return to a charger. Today, in the case of humanoid robots, usefulness may only last a few hours per charge. So how can that time be extended? There&#8217;s no simple answer because no single component will solve all problems, but the underlying basics remain the same — efficient processing, denser and faster memory, and optimized data movement between processors and memories. Frank Ferro, group director for product management at Cadence, explains why data hierarchies are so critical for physical AI, what can be done on the memory side to speed up read/write times, and how to maximize performance while keeping costs down.</p>
<p><iframe loading="lazy" width="480" height="270" src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/68oMarzN2xI" frameborder="0" allow="accelerometer; autoplay; encrypted-media; gyroscope; picture-in-picture" allowfullscreen></iframe></p>

		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Geoff Tate]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 09 Nov 2026 08:01:28 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Perspectives & Analysis]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[What's Coming Over The Horizon]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AMD]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Broadcom]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[data centers]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[interconnects]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Meta]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Microsoft]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[OCI-MSA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[optical interconnects]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283850</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Exponentially increasing AI compute performance requires exponential interconnect speeds.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-optical-super-highway-for-ai/">The Optical Superhighway For AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>There is much disagreement on the timing of the transition from copper interconnects to optical interconnects, but almost everyone agrees we are moving to all-optical interconnects over the next five years.</p>
<p>Optical interconnects already dominate scale-out. Optical interconnects will next move into scale-up by connecting racks in very large pods of hundreds to thousands of XPUs (AI compute units), then optical scale-up will replace copper within the rack.</p>
<p>Today, optical is dominated by pluggable transceivers mostly using single wavelengths (DR) with 2 or 4 fibers. Increasing performance using pluggables would require power-hungry, higher-speed PHYs (physical layer interfaces) and/or more fibers, which will drive up costs because connectors cost more than fibers.</p>
<p>To reduce power and cost while increasing bandwidth, the industry consensus is shifting toward multi-wavelengths per fiber with bidirectionality, using NPO (near-packaged optics), then CPO (co-packaged optics). Alternatives may develop, but the emerging consensus for the future of optical interconnect appears to be OCI-MSA.</p>
<h2>The optical superhighway for AI: OCI-MSA</h2>
<p><a href="https://oci-msa.org">OCI-MSA</a> (Optical Compute Interconnect Multi-Source Agreement) is an industry consortium recently formed for an open, interoperable optical interconnect spec for AI scale-up. Founding members include AMD, Broadcom, Meta, Microsoft, Nvidia, and OpenAI. In that mix are both GPU giants, the largest maker of custom AI compute, two of the hyperscalers, and one of the two Frontier Model companies. This is a big bandwagon. Of these, AMD, Broadcom, Meta, and Microsoft have made presentations on their plans for OCI-MSA at recent industry conferences.</p>
<p>The stated objectives for the OCI-MSA are:</p>
<ol>
<li>To create an open, interoperable specification for AI scale-up that breaks the constraints of proprietary interconnects to ensure seamless cross-vendor compatibility for hyperscale AI clusters.</li>
<li>To provide architectural guidance to transition the industry to low-power optical SerDes, delivering the latency, bandwidth, and energy efficiency required for next-generation AI.</li>
<li>To secure a scalable, multi-vendor AI supply chain, uniting hyperscale end-users and silicon innovators to reduce integration risk, accelerate development, and ensure long-term scale deployments without vendor lock-in.</li>
</ol>
<p>Drew Alduino (Meta&#8217;s director of optical infrastructure), Near Margalit (VP &amp; GM of Broadcom&#8217;s Optical Systems Division) &amp; Matthew Streshinsky (AMD senior director for photonics) were on a panel in September at the AI Infra Summit. &#8220;We want AMD GPUs talking to Broadcom switches,&#8221; Alduino said. &#8220;We want all of our compute and switches to be interoperable.”</p>
<p>Alduino went on to say:</p>
<ul>
<li>Meta wants to standardize as little as possible to achieve interoperability while making it possible for everyone to rapidly innovate.</li>
<li>Multi-wavelength means we don’t ever need faster SerDes for faster optical.</li>
<li>Meta wants OCI to look like Ethernet today.</li>
<li>Gen 1 OCI has 4 wavelengths of 50Gbps in each direction. Gen 2 is in definition and will double the bandwidth, while Gen 3 will double the bandwidth again.</li>
<li>200G × 16 wavelengths has already been shown to be &#8220;viable,&#8221; which is 16 × the bandwidth of Gen 1.</li>
</ul>
<p>As Near Margalit noted, what matters for interoperability is what appears on the fiber.</p>
<h2>OCI-MSA Gen 1</h2>
<p>The spec for Gen 1 of OCI-MSA can be found at <a href="https://oci-msa.org">oci-msa.org</a>.</p>
<p>OCI is workable for pluggables, NPO, CPO in the package, or CPO on the interposer.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283851" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig1.png?resize=1430%2C373&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1430" height="373" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig1.png?w=1430&amp;ssl=1 1430w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig1.png?resize=300%2C78&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig1.png?resize=1024%2C267&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig1.png?resize=768%2C200&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig1.png?resize=600%2C157&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: OCI-MSA Gen 1 has 8 channels per fiber, 4 in each direction. Source: oci-msa.org</strong></p>
<p>The OCI-MSA spec is 24 pages. The Optical specification provides for 8 wavelengths per fiber, with 4 in each direction. Each wavelength is 53.125Gbps NRZ. The wavelengths in the groups are spaced slightly more than 2nm apart. The reference optical fiber link model is based on 500m SMF-28 fiber with 2.5 dB total insertion loss, mainly driven by the connector losses in the link between transceivers. (The effective data rate is 50Gbps per wavelength after error correction and miscellaneous signaling.)</p>
<p>At OCP (Open Compute Project) in October, Rajiv Pancholy, director of hyperscale strategy &amp; products for Broadcom, talked about how OCI-MSA compares at equal bandwidths to alternatives. (See figure 2, below)</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283852" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=1430%2C744&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1430" height="744" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig2.jpg?w=1430&amp;ssl=1 1430w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=300%2C156&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=1024%2C533&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=768%2C400&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig2.jpg?resize=600%2C312&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Comparison of OCI power/reach versus alternatives. Source: Broadcom @ OCP</strong></p>
<p>As the table above shows, for a 200G aggregate bandwidth (one direction), OCI in CPO format is the best combination of low link power per bit and long reach. OCI has the reach of pluggables at 1/3 the power of a pluggable transceiver. And OCI has a scalable bandwidth roadmap.</p>
<h2>OCI-MSA Gen 2 and beyond</h2>
<p>At PECC (Photonic Enabled Cloud Computing) 2026 in October, AMD explained that there are numerous ways to increase OCI bandwidth for future generations:</p>
<ul>
<li>4 wavelengths → 8, 16, 32… (there is an upper limit given the width of the O band)</li>
<li>50 Gbps → 100, 200, 400, …</li>
<li>1 polarization → 2 polarizations</li>
<li>NRZ → PAM/QAM-n (advanced signaling likely required for the highest Gbps)</li>
</ul>
<p>The combination of all of these gives a scaling up of over 100×. If OCI doubles every 1 to 2 years, this gives decades of scalable runway. This is just the bandwidth per fiber. The number of fibers can increase, as well.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283853" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig3.jpg?resize=1430%2C649&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1430" height="649" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig3.jpg?w=1430&amp;ssl=1 1430w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig3.jpg?resize=300%2C136&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig3.jpg?resize=1024%2C465&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig3.jpg?resize=768%2C349&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig3.jpg?resize=600%2C272&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 3: OCI has numerous ways to continue to scale bandwidth. Source: AMD</strong></p>
<p>Microsoft at PECC also discussed its plans for OCI and the need for a common standard.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283854" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig4.jpg?resize=1430%2C700&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1430" height="700" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig4.jpg?w=1430&amp;ssl=1 1430w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig4.jpg?resize=300%2C147&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig4.jpg?resize=1024%2C501&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig4.jpg?resize=768%2C376&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Tate_Optical-Super-Highway-For-AI-fig4.jpg?resize=600%2C294&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 4: Microsoft will use OCI in CPO+NPO, then just CPO. Source: Microsoft</strong></p>
<p>Microsoft’s first wave of scale-up optical will use CPO on the switch and NPO with the accelerator. In the second wave, both ends will use CPO in scale-up.</p>
<h2>Conclusion</h2>
<p>So far, OCI-MSA is the only optical scale-up standard published and frequently discussed at multiple conferences (AI Infra/OCP/PECC) by multiple champions (AMD/Broadcom/Meta/Microsoft).</p>
<p>It seems likely that in 2027, we will see the first fully functional OCI Gen 1 implementations and perhaps interoperability demos between suppliers. If a “bandwagon” forms and multiple suppliers exist, OCI can become the Ethernet of optical interconnects, as Drew Alduino envisioned at AI Infra Summit.</p>
<p>Other standards could develop, or some players might choose to do a proprietary optical interconnect. But as of today, OCI-MSA is the frontrunner for the optical super highway for AI scale-up.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-optical-super-highway-for-ai/">The Optical Superhighway For AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>
